Table 5
Separating Fact from Fiction: Interrogating Stories, Evidence, and Context
1. Q: What is the central purpose of interrogating the stories we tell ourselves?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that people need to test the narratives they use to explain themselves and the world. A story may once have helped someone make sense of events, but it can become harmful when it no longer fits the available evidence or the context has changed. Interrogating a story means separating observable facts from explanations, checking how the facts are connected, looking for omitted information, and considering whether another person could test the reasoning. The aim is not to eliminate interpretation; interpretation is necessary for making meaning. The aim is to prevent an untested interpretation from being mistaken for reality and to build a more accurate, flexible account that supports better decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the chapter's four-part approach: establish facts, test how they fit together, search for missing details, and explain the reasoning to another person."
Key point:
- A useful story must remain answerable to evidence and current circumstances.
- The goal is a more accurate and actionable account, not a story that merely feels reassuring.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
2. Q: What does the chapter mean by the stories or narratives people construct?
A: Show answer
A personal story is the explanatory account a person builds from events, memories, assumptions, goals, and emotions. It connects separate observations into a plot: what happened, why it happened, what it means, and what is likely to happen next. The narrative can be helpful because it compresses complexity and guides action. It is not a neutral recording of every detail. People select information that seems relevant to their purpose, fill gaps with assumptions, and interpret ambiguous events through prior experience. Two people can observe the same event yet construct different stories because they have different information, interests, expectations, and frames of reference.
Study instruction:
- "Take one recent event and write separately what happened, what you inferred, and what you predict will happen next."
Key point:
- A narrative is an interpretation built from selected information, not a complete copy of reality.
- Different stories can arise from the same event without either person deliberately inventing facts.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
3. Q: Who or what is the chapter's Narrator, and why is it limited?
A: Show answer
The Narrator is the mind's largely automatic process of turning experience into a coherent account. It is usually trying to help a person navigate life, but it is not omniscient. It works with partial information, imperfect perception, memory, assumptions, and purposes. It may overlook details that do not fit the current plot, treat a guess as an explanation, or preserve a familiar identity even when circumstances have changed. Its limitations are not proof of irrationality or bad character; they are reasons to test conclusions. The practical discipline is to notice when the mind has moved from an observation to a causal explanation without showing the evidence for that move.
Study instruction:
- "When you notice a strong conclusion, identify which parts came from direct observation and which were supplied by your Narrator."
Key point:
- The mind automatically explains events, often before the evidence is complete.
- A well-intentioned Narrator can still produce an inaccurate or outdated story.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
4. Q: Why can a story that once helped someone become unhelpful?
A: Show answer
A story can become unhelpful when its assumptions no longer match the person's circumstances, goals, abilities, relationships, or environment. It may also have been inaccurate from the beginning but remained unchallenged because it produced a plausible explanation. For example, someone may define professional competence by a particular physical skill, then conclude that a career is over when illness or injury changes that skill. The conclusion may ignore other capabilities that still support the underlying purpose of the work. A story should therefore be evaluated for both factual accuracy and present usefulness. Usefulness does not justify denying facts; accuracy does not require preserving an interpretation that no longer helps guide constructive action.
Study instruction:
- "Choose an old belief about your work or identity and list the conditions that would make it outdated."
Key point:
- Stories are provisional models, not permanent definitions of a person or situation.
- Revising a story can restore options that an outdated interpretation concealed.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
5. Q: What practical sequence does the chapter recommend for testing a personal story?
A: Show answer
Begin by establishing the facts: identify what can be verified and distinguish it from explanations. Next, test whether the facts have been connected correctly. Ask whether the evidence really supports the claimed cause, whether alternative causes exist, and whether the conclusion depends on an assumption. Then look for missing details that could materially change the interpretation. Finally, where useful, externalize the reasoning by explaining it to another person. This sequence slows the jump from observation to certainty. It does not promise perfect knowledge; it creates a disciplined way to improve a story as better evidence becomes available.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the sequence to a current problem and record one fact, one interpretation, one missing detail, and one question for an impartial reviewer."
Key point:
- Facts, relationships, missing information, and external review are distinct checks.
- The process improves a story incrementally rather than guaranteeing certainty.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
6. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish what questions from why questions?
A: Show answer
A what question seeks the observable event or data: what was said, what occurred, what amount was recorded, or what action was taken. A why question seeks an explanation of cause or motive. The chapter uses Judea Pearl's observation about the Adam and Eve story to illustrate how people often answer a request for facts with an explanation. The same pattern appears in everyday reasoning: asked what happened, someone may immediately say that a colleague acted out of jealousy. The motive may be possible, but it is not the raw observation. Separating the two prevents a plausible explanation from being smuggled into the evidence record as if it were directly observed.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three why statements as neutral what statements before considering possible explanations."
Key point:
- What identifies the event; why proposes an explanation for it.
- A causal or motivational claim needs evidence beyond the event itself.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
7. Q: What counts as a fact in the chapter's method?
A: Show answer
A fact is a proposition that can be checked against observable evidence or a reliable record. Examples include a dated email, a measured result, a documented decision, a recorded transaction, or a statement that can be independently confirmed. A fact is not simply a thought that feels vivid, a confident memory, or an explanation repeated by several people. Facts can still be incomplete, mismeasured, or wrongly recorded, so verification may require checking the original source, context, timing, and method. The chapter's emphasis on facts is not that every fact is obvious; it is that claims should be open to confirmation or correction rather than protected by confidence alone.
Study instruction:
- "For a difficult claim, identify the original record or observation that would allow another person to verify it."
Key point:
- A fact is evidence-checkable, not merely strongly believed.
- Verification includes checking the source and conditions under which the information was produced.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
8. Q: Why is asking 'What is your source of information?' such a powerful test?
A: Show answer
The question exposes whether a belief comes from a direct observation, a reliable record, a second-hand report, an inference, or imagination. The chapter recounts Mary Karr's mentor asking this question when Karr brought a frightening or troubling thought; Karr often recognized that she had thought the claim up herself. The question is useful because the mind can generate explanations automatically when information is missing. Asking for a source interrupts that process and makes the origin of a claim visible. It does not mean every unsourced thought is false; it means the thought should be labelled as unverified until evidence is obtained.
Study instruction:
- "For each worrying prediction, write its information source and classify it as direct, reported, inferred, or imagined."
Key point:
- The source of a belief is part of evaluating its reliability.
- An unsupported thought should not be treated as an established fact.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
9. Q: How should fact, opinion, prediction, theory, hypothesis, and conjecture be distinguished?
A: Show answer
A fact is a claim supported by verifiable evidence. An opinion expresses a judgment or preference. A prediction describes an expected future outcome and remains uncertain until tested. A hypothesis is a proposed explanation or relationship that can be examined against evidence. A theory is a more developed explanatory framework supported by a body of reasoning and evidence, although it remains open to refinement. Conjecture is a tentative claim that has not been adequately established. These categories are not interchangeable: a prediction can later be confirmed, and a hypothesis can be supported or rejected, but neither becomes a fact merely because it sounds plausible. Labelling the status of each claim helps calibrate confidence.
Study instruction:
- "Take five statements from a discussion and label each as fact, opinion, prediction, hypothesis, or conjecture, explaining what evidence could change its status."
Key point:
- The strength and type of a claim determine what evidence is needed.
- Confidence should match the claim's evidential status.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
10. Q: How can a thought masquerade as a fact?
A: Show answer
A thought can feel factual when it is familiar, emotionally intense, repeated, or consistent with an existing belief. A person may think, 'My colleague ignored my message because she wants me to fail,' when the observable fact is only that no reply has arrived. The mind has added an intention and a cause. The explanation may later prove correct, but the current evidence does not establish it. Repetition can make the explanation feel more certain without adding new information. A useful correction is to state the observation in neutral language, identify the inference separately, and list what evidence would confirm or disconfirm it.
Study instruction:
- "Convert an emotionally charged conclusion into a sentence that contains only observable details, then write the inference separately."
Key point:
- Familiarity and emotional force do not convert an inference into a fact.
- Describe the observation before assigning motive or cause.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
11. Q: Why does the chapter treat emotions as interpretations rather than objective proof about events?
A: Show answer
The chapter draws on Lisa Feldman Barrett's account that bodily sensations and affective signals are interpreted and categorized in context. Feeling anxious, angry, or rejected is a real experience, but the feeling alone does not prove the external explanation attached to it. A racing heart may be labelled fear, excitement, or readiness depending on context and learned concepts. Similarly, feeling disrespected does not by itself establish that another person intended disrespect. This distinction should not be used to dismiss emotions; feelings are important information about a person's experience. It prevents the additional mistake of treating the emotional label or assumed cause as independently verified evidence.
Study instruction:
- "When an emotion is strong, record the bodily sensation, the label you gave it, the event you associate with it, and what evidence supports that association."
Key point:
- An emotion is real as an experience but is not conclusive proof of another person's motive.
- Separating sensation, label, and explanation creates room for more accurate interpretation.
Source links:
- Barrett: The Theory of Constructed Emotion
12. Q: How can stress, fatigue, or excitement distort the facts a person believes they perceived?
A: Show answer
Stress, fatigue, and excitement can affect attention, memory, interpretation, and confidence. A person may notice only part of a conversation, misremember wording, overlook context, or interpret an ambiguous expression as hostile. These conditions do not mean the perception is necessarily wrong; they increase the reason to check it. Before relying on a memory, ask whether the event was observed directly, whether notes or records exist, whether the person was distracted, and whether another observer can corroborate it. For consequential decisions, use contemporaneous documentation rather than confidence in recollection alone.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recent disagreement and identify whether fatigue, stress, time pressure, or divided attention could have affected what you noticed."
Key point:
- Perception is fallible, especially under conditions that strain attention.
- Important recollections should be checked against contemporaneous evidence where possible.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
13. Q: What is the difference between an observation and a conclusion?
A: Show answer
An observation reports what was seen, heard, measured, or recorded. A conclusion explains what the observation means. 'The supplier delivered three days after the agreed date' is an observation if the contract and delivery record confirm it. 'The supplier does not care about the project' is a conclusion about motive. Conclusions are necessary for decisions, but they should be built from evidence and tested against alternatives. A conclusion can be strong when multiple independent facts support it, or weak when it rests on one ambiguous event. Keeping the two levels separate makes discussions more precise and allows others to challenge the reasoning without disputing the underlying facts.
Study instruction:
- "For one workplace issue, write an observation column and a conclusion column; do not place motive or causation in the observation column."
Key point:
- Observations describe evidence; conclusions interpret it.
- A sound conclusion explains how the evidence supports the interpretation.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
14. Q: How can a person check whether an event was perceived accurately?
A: Show answer
Reconstruct the event from the best available evidence rather than relying only on the first memory. Check exact wording, timing, sequence, location, participants, and relevant records. Ask what conditions could have affected perception, such as noise, distraction, stress, or incomplete visibility. If the matter is important, seek independent accounts without leading witnesses toward a preferred answer. Preserve differences between accounts rather than forcing them into one version prematurely. The goal is not to demand impossible certainty; it is to identify what is known, what is disputed, and what remains unknown before drawing a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Create a short verification checklist for a disputed event covering time, source, wording, sequence, corroboration, and uncertainty."
Key point:
- Memory should be treated as evidence that may need corroboration.
- A fair review preserves uncertainty instead of hiding it.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
15. Q: How should relevant facts be separated from irrelevant facts?
A: Show answer
A fact is relevant when it bears on the question being decided or helps distinguish between competing explanations. Irrelevant facts may be true but do not materially support the conclusion. The chapter warns against arranging details to produce a preferred story. A practical method is to state the decision question first, list all available facts, and then explain why each fact matters. Include evidence that weakens the preferred explanation as well as evidence that supports it. Avoid excluding inconvenient information merely because it complicates the narrative. Relevance should be judged by the question and the causal logic, not by whether the fact makes the story more comfortable.
Study instruction:
- "Take a decision you are considering and annotate each piece of evidence as directly relevant, indirectly relevant, or currently irrelevant, with a reason."
Key point:
- Truth alone does not make a detail relevant to every decision.
- Evidence that challenges the preferred story must remain visible.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
16. Q: What does the quinoa example teach about sketchy data and hasty conclusions?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how rising quinoa prices and lower consumption among some Andean growers were presented as evidence that global demand had harmed the original producers. The account was incomplete: higher crop prices also increased income for many growers, who could choose different foods. The same observed change could therefore support more than one interpretation. The lesson is not that the first report was necessarily malicious; it is that a partial set of facts can produce a dramatic but misleading story when omitted context is not examined. Before repeating a causal claim, check the data's scope, who was measured, what changed, and what alternative explanations fit the evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Use the quinoa example to identify the observed facts, the initial causal claim, the omitted economic context, and the revised interpretation."
Key point:
- A partial data set can support a compelling but incomplete narrative.
- Check who benefits, who is measured, and what relevant context is missing.
Source links:
- Negri: Correlation Is Not Causation, Yet
17. Q: Why does correlation between two events not establish that one caused the other?
A: Show answer
Two variables can move together because one affects the other, because the direction is reversed, or because a third factor affects both. The chapter illustrates this with sunglasses, ice cream, and drowning: all may increase in warmer weather, but buying ice cream does not cause drowning. Weather is a common factor. A correlation is a clue worth investigating, not a complete causal explanation. To support a causal claim, examine timing, plausible mechanisms, alternative causes, selection effects, and the research design. In observational settings, causal inference may be possible, but it requires explicit assumptions and careful analysis rather than a simple comparison of two trends.
Study instruction:
- "For a correlation you encounter in a report, draw at least one plausible common cause and one alternative causal direction before accepting the explanation."
Key point:
- Association is not sufficient evidence of causation.
- A causal claim needs a plausible design and an examination of alternative explanations.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
- Negri: Correlation Is Not Causation, Yet
18. Q: What is a hidden common cause, and how can it create a misleading story?
A: Show answer
A hidden common cause is a factor that influences two observed variables and makes them appear causally connected. In the chapter's weather example, warmer conditions can increase both ice-cream sales and swimming exposure, while the latter may affect drowning risk. If the common factor is omitted, someone may invent a direct relationship between the two visible variables. In research, this is often called confounding. The remedy is not to add every possible variable mechanically; it is to use subject knowledge and a causal model to identify plausible common causes, measure them where possible, and explain what remains unobserved.
Study instruction:
- "For a claimed relationship between X and Y, ask what third variable could influence both and how you would measure it."
Key point:
- A common cause can create a non-causal association between two variables.
- Confounder selection requires causal reasoning, not blind adjustment.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
- Negri: Correlation Is Not Causation, Yet
19. Q: How can someone test alternative explanations before accepting a causal story?
A: Show answer
Start by writing the preferred explanation as a testable claim. Then generate plausible alternatives, including reverse direction, common causes, selection effects, measurement error, and missing context. For each explanation, identify what evidence should be present if it were true and what evidence would count against it. Prefer tests that could distinguish among the alternatives rather than merely confirm the preferred story. Check whether the sequence of events is consistent with the proposed cause and whether the mechanism is plausible. Where evidence is limited, state the conclusion provisionally and identify what further information would materially change it.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a three-column test: explanation, evidence expected if true, and evidence that would weaken it."
Key point:
- A strong test tries to distinguish explanations, not merely collect supporting examples.
- Alternative hypotheses make reasoning more robust.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
- Negri: Correlation Is Not Causation, Yet
20. Q: What additional causal errors should be considered beyond a hidden common cause?
A: Show answer
A causal story can be wrong because the direction is reversed, because the sample is selected in a way that distorts the relationship, because measurement is inaccurate, or because the analysis controls for the wrong variables. For example, a person may assume that low morale causes poor performance when repeated missed targets may also be causing low morale. Selection bias can arise when only certain cases are observed. In more advanced causal models, controlling for a mediator or collider can introduce bias rather than remove it. The chapter's practical lesson is to question how the dots are connected; technical causal analysis adds that the choice of variables and design matters as much as the observed association.
Study instruction:
- "For an important causal claim, test reverse causation, selection bias, measurement error, and whether any proposed control variable could distort the relationship."
Key point:
- Causal mistakes include more than confusing correlation with direct cause.
- Adding controls without a causal rationale can make an analysis worse.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
21. Q: How can a simple causal diagram help interrogate a story?
A: Show answer
A causal diagram makes assumptions visible by representing variables as nodes and proposed causal influences as arrows. If a person claims that X causes Y, the diagram can prompt questions about common causes, intermediate steps, reverse direction, and variables that were not measured. It is not proof that the arrows are correct; it is a disciplined way to expose the story's structure so others can challenge it. A diagram should be based on subject knowledge and the timing of events, not drawn merely to justify a desired result. Its value is that an implicit explanation becomes an explicit model with assumptions that can be examined.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a small directed diagram for one workplace or personal problem and label every arrow as an assumption, observation, or supported mechanism."
Key point:
- A causal diagram exposes the structure of an explanation.
- The diagram itself is a hypothesis and must be evaluated.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
22. Q: Does the warning about correlation mean observational evidence is useless?
A: Show answer
No. Observational evidence can reveal patterns, help generate hypotheses, and contribute to causal inference when supported by a credible design and explicit assumptions. The warning is that an association alone is insufficient. Researchers may use natural experiments, longitudinal evidence, matching, adjustment, or other strategies, but each approach has limitations. For example, matching can improve comparability on measured characteristics but cannot automatically remove bias from unmeasured factors. The responsible conclusion reports the design, assumptions, uncertainty, and limits on generalization. In everyday reasoning, the equivalent is to use patterns as clues while resisting the claim that the pattern alone proves the story.
Study instruction:
- "When reading an observational study, identify the exposure, outcome, design, measured confounders, unmeasured risks, and stated limitations."
Key point:
- Observational evidence can be informative without being conclusive.
- Causal confidence depends on design and assumptions, not on correlation alone.
Source links:
- Negri: Correlation Is Not Causation, Yet
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
23. Q: What is meant by the chapter's idea of the hidden half?
A: Show answer
The hidden half refers to the information about a situation that is not currently known or visible. People often make decisions from partial data because the mind wants a complete explanation. Yet missing factors may be as important as the facts already collected. A child's homework difficulty may involve a learning problem as well as attraction to video games; a promotion decision may reflect performance evidence that an observer has not seen. The correct response is not to invent an unlimited list of possibilities. It is to identify the most consequential unknowns, assess how they could change the conclusion, and decide what additional information is worth obtaining.
Study instruction:
- "For a current problem, list the three unknowns most likely to change your interpretation and decide how each could be checked."
Key point:
- Unknown information can materially alter an apparently obvious explanation.
- Good reasoning identifies important gaps instead of filling them with guesses.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
24. Q: What is a metaphor, and why does the chapter treat metaphors as part of reasoning?
A: Show answer
A metaphor uses one domain of experience to understand another. It does more than decorate language: it can organize attention, highlight selected similarities, and suggest what actions seem appropriate. Calling a manager a 'bear' may make avoidance seem natural; calling a colleague a 'rock' may encourage others to rely on that person without noticing overload. The metaphor supplies a frame that can influence perception even when the speaker does not intend to make a formal argument. The chapter therefore recommends identifying the metaphor behind a story and examining what it reveals, what it hides, and whether a different frame would better fit the evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a metaphor you use about a person, job, or problem and write down the assumptions and actions it encourages."
Key point:
- Metaphors are cognitive frames that can guide interpretation and behavior.
- A metaphor should be tested for what it leaves out as well as what it highlights.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
- Thibodeau and Boroditsky: Metaphors We Think With
25. Q: What benefits do metaphors provide when people try to understand complex problems?
A: Show answer
Metaphors compress complex ideas into familiar relationships, allowing people to communicate quickly and generate new perspectives. A map can help explain a strategy, a bridge can describe a relationship between concepts, and a machine can help someone reason about interacting parts. These frames can support memory, teaching, and creative problem-solving. The benefit depends on whether the mapped features genuinely fit the target situation. A useful metaphor makes an aspect of a problem easier to see; it does not establish that every feature of the source domain applies. Good users of metaphor treat it as a thinking aid, not as a substitute for evidence or analysis.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a metaphor that helps explain a complex issue and state precisely which similarities are useful and which should not be carried over."
Key point:
- Metaphors can make abstract relationships easier to understand.
- The analogy is useful only within the limits of the similarities it supports.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
26. Q: How can a metaphor influence decisions without the person noticing?
A: Show answer
A metaphor can activate a structured set of associations and make certain responses feel natural. Describing crime as a 'beast' may lead people toward containment or force, while describing it as a 'virus' may lead them toward prevention and treatment. The frame can affect which information people seek and which solutions they consider, even if they later explain their choice using other reasons. Experimental research has found that metaphorical framing can influence reasoning about complex social problems. This does not mean language determines every decision; it means wording can be one influence among prior beliefs, evidence, values, and context.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a problem statement using two different metaphors and compare the solutions each frame makes more visible."
Key point:
- Metaphorical framing can shape the options people notice.
- A decision should be justified by evidence and goals, not by the frame alone.
Source links:
- Thibodeau and Boroditsky: Metaphors We Think With
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
27. Q: What is the main limitation of committing too strongly to one metaphor?
A: Show answer
A metaphor can narrow attention to the features it makes salient and hide features that do not fit. If a colleague is treated as a 'machine', managers may focus on output and ignore fatigue, learning needs, or personal constraints. If a difficult supervisor is described as a 'bear', the metaphor may encourage avoidance rather than a careful assessment of specific behavior and available ways to interact. Once a metaphor becomes familiar, people may interpret new evidence through it and reinforce the original frame. The correction is to state the literal facts, test whether the analogy still fits, and consider another frame or no metaphor at all.
Study instruction:
- "For one persistent label, list three facts that fit it and three facts it cannot explain."
Key point:
- A metaphor highlights some features while obscuring others.
- Do not let a convenient label replace observation of the actual situation.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
- Thibodeau and Boroditsky: Metaphors We Think With
28. Q: How should someone strip away a metaphor when interrogating a story?
A: Show answer
First, identify the metaphorical label or image. Second, explain exactly what comparison is being made. Third, ask which observable behaviors support the comparison and which contradict it. Fourth, restate the situation without the metaphor using concrete descriptions, dates, actions, and outcomes. Finally, test whether the proposed response still makes sense when the label is removed. For example, replace 'she is a sales machine' with measurable information about sales, customer mix, workload, and recent changes. Literal restatement does not eliminate judgment; it gives judgment a clearer evidential foundation.
Study instruction:
- "Take one metaphorical description and rewrite it as a neutral account using observable actions, measures, and context."
Key point:
- Literal restatement reveals the evidence hidden behind a label.
- A decision should survive removal of the metaphor that first made it seem obvious.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
29. Q: How can a metaphor become a self-fulfilling prophecy?
A: Show answer
A metaphor can shape expectations and behavior in ways that help produce the outcome it predicts. If a student is repeatedly labelled incapable, teachers may provide fewer challenging opportunities, the student may lose confidence, and performance may decline. If a colleague is treated as endlessly dependable, work may be assigned without checking capacity, increasing the chance of overload. The original label then appears confirmed even though the treatment helped create the result. This is not proof that every metaphor causes its predicted outcome; it shows why labels should be examined for their behavioral consequences and for feedback loops between expectation and action.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one label that may influence how people are treated and trace the possible chain from label to expectation, behavior, and outcome."
Key point:
- Stories can change behavior and thereby influence the evidence that follows.
- Check whether a label is describing a pattern or helping to create it.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
- Thibodeau and Boroditsky: Metaphors We Think With
30. Q: What are language traps, and why do word choices matter?
A: Show answer
Language traps occur when habitual or emotionally loaded wording embeds assumptions that influence how a person sees a situation. Phrases such as 'I have to', 'they always', 'nothing works', or 'I failed' may turn a specific event into a broad identity or inevitability claim. Words also signal whether a person sees an activity as a burden, an opportunity, or a choice. The chapter does not suggest that changing words magically changes external conditions. It argues that more precise wording can expose assumptions, reduce unnecessary resignation, and make alternative actions easier to consider.
Study instruction:
- "Underline absolute or disempowering phrases in a personal account and replace each with a more specific statement that preserves the facts."
Key point:
- Language can reinforce the story a person tells about circumstances and identity.
- Precise wording creates room for more accurate choices without denying constraints.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
31. Q: How can the same event tell different stories depending on the words used?
A: Show answer
Different word choices can frame the same event with different implications. 'I have to speak at a conference' emphasizes obligation, while 'I get to speak at a conference' emphasizes access or opportunity. Both may describe the same scheduled activity, but they direct attention toward different aspects of it. Similarly, 'the employee missed the deadline' is more specific than 'the employee is unreliable'; the second turns one event into a character judgment. Language should therefore be checked for scope, certainty, agency, and implied motive. The most useful wording is neither artificially positive nor harsh; it is accurate enough to support a fair decision.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a recent complaint in neutral language and then in constructive language; confirm that neither version changes the underlying facts."
Key point:
- Words can turn a specific event into a broad judgment.
- Choose language that is accurate, proportionate, and action-oriented.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
- Thibodeau and Boroditsky: Metaphors We Think With
32. Q: What is the practical difference between saying 'I have to' and 'I get to'?
A: Show answer
The chapter's example shows that changing 'I have to speak' into 'I get to speak' helped the narrator recognize that he had chosen the work and was being paid for an opportunity many people would value. The wording redirected attention toward choice and gratitude. It is not a universal instruction to replace every obligation with positive language. Some duties are imposed, and some circumstances involve real hardship or limited choice. The useful practice is to ask whether the original phrase accurately represents the situation and whether it hides choices, values, or benefits that are also true. Reframing should add perspective, not erase reality.
Study instruction:
- "For one task you resent, distinguish what is genuinely compulsory, what you chose, and what value or benefit may also be present."
Key point:
- A language shift can reveal agency and value that a complaint overlooks.
- Reframing is useful only when it remains truthful about constraints.
Source links:
- Lakoff: Mapping the Brain's Metaphor Circuitry
33. Q: What does the map-and-territory analogy teach about personal stories?
A: Show answer
A map represents selected features of a territory for a particular purpose; it is not the territory itself. Personal stories work similarly. They simplify experience so people can navigate it, but every story leaves out details and reflects a purpose. A road map, weather map, and topographic map can all be accurate for different tasks. A story that helps someone prepare for a presentation may be inadequate for evaluating a long-term career decision. The danger arises when a person mistakes one representation for the whole reality or forgets why the representation was created.
Study instruction:
- "Name the purpose of a personal story and identify at least two aspects of reality it leaves outside the frame."
Key point:
- A story is a selective model of reality, not reality in its entirety.
- Judge a story by its purpose and the details it omits.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
34. Q: How can a map be accurate for one purpose but misleading for another?
A: Show answer
Accuracy is partly task-dependent because different maps preserve different information. A road map prioritizes routes and connections; a topographic map emphasizes elevation and terrain; a weather map describes atmospheric conditions. Using a weather map to navigate a road journey is a category error, not evidence that the weather map is badly made. Personal models should be assessed in the same way. A simple rule may help with a routine task but fail when the decision concerns safety, relationships, or a different population. Ask what the model was designed to represent and whether that is the question now being asked.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one rule you use and specify the decisions for which it is suitable and the decisions for which it is not."
Key point:
- A model's fitness depends on the decision it is meant to support.
- Do not judge or apply a model without checking its intended purpose.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
35. Q: Why must a personal story be reconsidered when its context changes?
A: Show answer
A story's assumptions may depend on conditions that are no longer present. Changes in health, staffing, customer needs, regulations, technology, family circumstances, location, season, or economic conditions can alter the relationship between an action and its outcome. A strategy that worked in one environment may fail in another because the relevant constraints and incentives have changed. Reconsidering a story does not mean abandoning every principle whenever circumstances shift. It means identifying which assumptions remain stable, which have changed, and whether the original explanation still predicts what happens under current conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the conditions under which a strategy originally worked with the conditions that exist now; mark each assumption as stable, changed, or unknown."
Key point:
- Context is part of the explanation, not background decoration.
- A once-useful story must be retested when important conditions change.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
36. Q: Why should successful principles and best practices not be applied mechanically everywhere?
A: Show answer
Principles often summarize what worked in a particular organization, market, culture, or period. They may be transferable when the receiving context shares the relevant conditions, but they can fail when those conditions differ. The chapter's LEGO example describes an innovation strategy imported from other industries that encouraged the company to move away from its core building-block audience. The principles were not necessarily universally wrong; they were poorly fitted to the specific customer and business context. Before copying a best practice, identify the mechanism that made it work, the assumptions it depends on, and whether those assumptions exist in the new setting.
Study instruction:
- "Before adopting a celebrated practice, list its original context, success mechanism, required conditions, and differences in your own setting."
Key point:
- A principle is not a context-free guarantee of success.
- Transfer the mechanism only after testing whether its assumptions travel.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
37. Q: What does the LEGO example illustrate about strategy and context?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts LEGO's late-1990s difficulties and the use of an innovation strategy associated with successful companies in other industries. The effort to become more innovative moved the company away from its core audience of children who enjoyed building with the bricks. The result was damaging, and recovery involved reconnecting with core products, customer input, and more disciplined production. The example illustrates that a strategy can sound sophisticated and have worked elsewhere yet still be unsuitable for a particular organization. It also shows why customer understanding and operational fit matter when translating general principles into specific decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a strategy you have seen copied from another organization and identify which customer, capability, or market assumptions may not transfer."
Key point:
- A strategy must fit the organization's actual customers and capabilities.
- A successful example elsewhere is evidence to investigate, not a universal prescription.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
38. Q: Which contextual variables should be checked before deciding that a story still applies?
A: Show answer
The chapter lists factors such as demographics, staffing, availability, style, customer base, competitors, education, life stage, location, season, time of day, economic performance, major life events, election cycles, regulations, taxation, weather, and health. The list is illustrative rather than exhaustive. The relevant variables depend on the problem. For a staffing strategy, changes in skills, workload, and labor availability may matter. For a product decision, customer needs, competitors, and purchasing conditions may matter. The reviewer should select variables that plausibly affect the mechanism behind the story instead of treating every contextual detail as equally important.
Study instruction:
- "For a decision you are reviewing, identify the five contextual variables most likely to change the expected outcome and explain why."
Key point:
- Context checks should focus on variables that can alter the causal mechanism.
- A broad list is a prompt for analysis, not a checklist to apply mechanically.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
39. Q: Why can one overlooked variable radically change an outcome?
A: Show answer
A decision may depend on several interacting conditions rather than one dominant factor. If a person assumes that a strategy works because of a single cause, a change in another variable may invalidate the prediction. For example, a sales method may work when customers have high disposable income but fail during a downturn; a staffing rule may work with experienced employees but create errors when a team is new. A small contextual change can matter greatly when it affects a key assumption or bottleneck. The correct response is to identify which variables are causally important and monitor them rather than assume that yesterday's pattern will continue unchanged.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the key assumptions behind a forecast and run a simple sensitivity check showing how the result changes if one important variable moves."
Key point:
- Outcomes often depend on interacting conditions.
- Monitor the assumptions that have the greatest influence on the result.
Source links:
- MacIntyre: The Map and the Territory
- Negri: Correlation Is Not Causation, Yet
40. Q: What does the chapter mean when it says science has a subjective side and an objective side?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes the tentative, creative work of generating questions and hypotheses from the public testing and evaluation of claims. Researchers may use intuition, experience, hunches, or prior beliefs to decide what to investigate. Those ideas are subjective starting points, not proof. When researchers present a scientific claim, they must describe methods and evidence so others can assess the reasoning. Objectivity is therefore a discipline of making claims testable and open to criticism, not a guarantee that scientists have no assumptions or personal perspectives.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the hypothesis-generation stage from the evidence-testing stage in a research example and identify what makes the second stage open to scrutiny."
Key point:
- Subjective ideas can initiate inquiry; evidence and transparent methods test them.
- Scientific objectivity is strengthened by criticism, verification, and disclosure of methods.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
41. Q: How should a hypothesis be treated before sufficient evidence is available?
A: Show answer
A hypothesis is a proposed explanation that guides inquiry. It should be specific enough to generate observable implications and should remain open to rejection or revision. The fact that a hypothesis motivates an experiment does not make it true, and the effort invested in it does not create an obligation to defend it. Researchers should specify what results would support the hypothesis, what results would weaken it, and what limitations affect the test. In everyday life, the same discipline applies to explanations about work, relationships, or performance: state the claim in a way that evidence could challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Write one hypothesis about a problem and specify an observation that would count against it, not only an observation that would support it."
Key point:
- A useful hypothesis can be tested and potentially rejected.
- Investment in an explanation is not evidence for its truth.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
42. Q: What is confirmation bias, and how can it distort a personal story?
A: Show answer
Confirmation bias is the tendency to seek, interpret, or remember information in ways that favor an existing belief or hypothesis. A person who thinks a colleague is unreliable may notice every late response while overlooking completed work or circumstances that explain a delay. The bias can operate during information search, evaluation, assimilation, memory, and source selection. It need not involve deliberate dishonesty. The danger is that a story becomes increasingly convincing because the person filters evidence through it. A corrective process deliberately seeks disconfirming evidence, uses explicit criteria, and asks an impartial reviewer to examine the same information.
Study instruction:
- "For a belief you hold strongly, record one item of supporting evidence and actively search for the strongest credible evidence against it."
Key point:
- Confirmation bias favors evidence that fits an existing belief.
- A fair test searches for disconfirming information as well as supporting information.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
43. Q: Why do goals and desired outcomes influence the way people interpret evidence?
A: Show answer
People do not evaluate information in a vacuum. A desired outcome can affect which facts receive attention, how much weight is assigned to them, and which explanations feel acceptable. The chapter's household-budget example shows how two people can look at the same numbers but disagree about what spending is reasonable because they have different priorities. In a workplace, a manager who wants to protect a project may interpret missed targets differently from an auditor who is assessing control risk. Recognizing the influence of goals does not mean all interpretations are equally valid; it means the evaluator should disclose the decision objective and test whether it is distorting the evidence.
Study instruction:
- "For a disputed decision, state what outcome you want and then review the evidence as if you had no personal stake in that outcome."
Key point:
- Goals can influence how facts are selected and interpreted.
- Stating the desired outcome helps reveal possible motivated reasoning.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
44. Q: How can someone deliberately look for evidence that would disconfirm a preferred conclusion?
A: Show answer
Translate the conclusion into a testable claim and ask what would have to be true for it to be wrong. Search for cases that do not fit the pattern, records that contradict the timeline, alternative explanations, and independent sources with different incentives. Avoid setting a test that only the preferred explanation can pass. Where possible, define evaluation criteria before reviewing the results so that the standard is not changed after seeing inconvenient evidence. A disconfirming result does not automatically prove the opposite conclusion; it may show that the original claim is too broad, conditional, or uncertain.
Study instruction:
- "Write a falsification question for one strongly held belief and identify the best source of evidence that could answer it."
Key point:
- A belief is more credible when it survives serious attempts to challenge it.
- Disconfirmation may narrow or qualify a claim rather than simply reverse it.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
45. Q: What does it mean to show your work when explaining a conclusion?
A: Show answer
Showing your work means making the reasoning chain visible: identify the facts, explain the assumptions, describe how the facts were connected, and state why the conclusion follows. The chapter uses scientific communication as an analogy: researchers document methods and submit claims to scrutiny rather than expecting others to accept a result because the researcher is confident. In everyday decisions, explaining the chain exposes gaps that may have remained implicit. It also allows colleagues to locate the exact point of disagreement: the data, the assumption, the causal link, or the value judgment.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a short reasoning note for a decision that lists evidence, assumptions, alternative explanations, and the conclusion drawn from them."
Key point:
- A conclusion is easier to evaluate when its reasoning is visible.
- Showing your work invites correction of specific assumptions rather than argument about impressions.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
46. Q: Which questions help interrogate the logic of a personal or professional story?
A: Show answer
Useful questions include: What assumptions am I making? What is the goal? What do I want to be true? Am I being objective? What would an impartial observer say? How did I draw this conclusion? Is it testable, and how could it be verified? Can the conclusion be drawn from the facts available? These questions are effective because they examine both the evidence and the reasoning that connects it. They also reveal whether a person is protecting an identity, defending a decision, or avoiding a difficult possibility. The questions should be used to clarify, not to create endless doubt about every ordinary judgment.
Study instruction:
- "Select five questions from the list and apply them to a decision that has important consequences."
Key point:
- Interrogation examines assumptions, goals, evidence, and logical connections.
- The purpose is disciplined review, not perpetual indecision.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
47. Q: Why is external review useful when a person explains a story to someone else?
A: Show answer
Explaining a story to another person forces the speaker to state assumptions and reasoning that may have remained implicit. A listener may notice a missing step, an unsupported motive, a contradiction, or a fact the speaker treated as irrelevant. The value comes from a genuinely independent perspective, not merely from finding someone who agrees. External review also has limits: reviewers can share the same biases, lack relevant expertise, or be influenced by power relationships. For consequential matters, choose reviewers who understand the subject, disclose relevant interests, and are willing to challenge the reasoning respectfully.
Study instruction:
- "Ask a trusted but independent reviewer to identify the weakest assumption in your explanation and one alternative account you have not considered."
Key point:
- Explaining a story makes hidden reasoning easier to examine.
- Choose reviewers for independence and relevant knowledge, not agreement.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
48. Q: How do peer review, reproducibility, and replication strengthen the testing of stories?
A: Show answer
Peer review allows other specialists to question the evidence, methods, analysis, and conclusions before or after publication. Reproducibility concerns obtaining consistent computational results using the same data, methods, code, and conditions. Replicability concerns obtaining consistent results in new studies addressing the same question. These practices do not make every result certain, and a failed replication does not automatically prove fraud or falsity. They create opportunities to identify errors, reveal uncertainty, and refine explanations. The broader lesson for everyday reasoning is to preserve enough information for another person to check how a conclusion was reached.
Study instruction:
- "For a published claim, look for the methods, data availability, independent replication, and limitations before treating it as settled."
Key point:
- Independent scrutiny helps detect weaknesses that an original investigator may miss.
- Reproducibility and replication are related but distinct tests.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
49. Q: What does the coach's response to the missed budget teach about facts and factors?
A: Show answer
In the chapter, the narrator initially explained a budget shortfall by pointing to external conditions such as retail traffic and fuel prices. His coach acknowledged that these were real factors but challenged the claim that they fully determined the outcome. She asked what aspect of his leadership had contributed to the result. The distinction is important: a factor can influence an outcome without being its sole or decisive cause. The exercise did not deny the external environment; it restored attention to a variable the narrator could examine and change. A more complete explanation can recognize external constraints while still testing personal or organizational contributions.
Study instruction:
- "For a disappointing result, distinguish external factors, internal decisions, and the evidence for each contribution."
Key point:
- A factor may influence an outcome without explaining it completely.
- A fuller causal account can reveal actions within one's control.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
50. Q: How can someone distinguish personal responsibility from blaming themselves for everything?
A: Show answer
Interrogating a story requires identifying the person's actual contribution without assuming that every outcome is personally controllable. Separate controllable decisions, influenceable conditions, and external constraints. Then ask what evidence links each factor to the result. A manager may have responsibility for planning and communication while also facing a market shock; an employee may control preparation but not a sudden policy change. Taking responsibility means acknowledging one's real part and deciding what can be improved. It does not mean accepting unsupported blame, ignoring structural causes, or treating every adverse event as a personal failure.
Study instruction:
- "Map a recent outcome into controllable, influenceable, and external factors, and identify one evidence-based action in each relevant category."
Key point:
- Accountability is specific and evidence-based, not unlimited self-blame.
- A balanced explanation includes both personal agency and external conditions.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
51. Q: What does Michele Cushatt's experience teach about identity and changing circumstances?
A: Show answer
Michele Cushatt's career as a speaker and speaking coach was built around a strong voice and polished diction. After repeated treatment for tongue cancer and major reconstruction, her voice and speech changed, and some listeners responded cruelly. She initially interpreted the change as the end of her career as a communicator. By listing what she believed made a good communicator and testing each item, she recognized that vocal quality and perfect enunciation were not the whole of communication. Empathy, truthfulness, insight, and passion remained. The lesson is to distinguish a valued purpose from one familiar method or attribute used to pursue it.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a role or identity you associate with one particular skill and list other capabilities that could still serve its underlying purpose."
Key point:
- A change in one ability does not automatically erase the broader capacity or purpose.
- Test the definition of success before concluding that a path is closed.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
52. Q: How can someone separate a role, a skill, and the deeper purpose behind an identity?
A: Show answer
A role is a social or professional position, a skill is a capability used in that role, and a purpose is the value or outcome the person seeks to create. Confusing these levels can make a change in one skill feel like the loss of an entire identity. Michele's story illustrates the difference between being a great speaker in the narrow sense of having a powerful voice and being a communicator who connects with people through insight and empathy. A person should identify which elements are essential to the purpose and which are methods that can be adapted. This opens alternatives without pretending that the loss or limitation is insignificant.
Study instruction:
- "Write one identity statement, separate the role, skills, and purpose within it, and identify at least two alternative ways to serve the purpose."
Key point:
- Purpose can remain meaningful when a familiar method or skill changes.
- A flexible identity is more resilient than one defined by a single attribute.
Source links:
-
53. Q: What is a practical evidence log for testing a difficult story?
A: Show answer
An evidence log records the claim being tested, the observable facts, the source and date of each fact, the interpretation attached to it, the confidence level, alternative explanations, and unresolved questions. It should preserve evidence that supports and challenges the preferred story. Distinguish original records from summaries and recollections, and note whether information is independently corroborated. An evidence log is not a substitute for formal investigation or professional advice in high-stakes cases. Its purpose is to prevent memory, repetition, or emotional intensity from quietly changing what is known as the story develops.
Study instruction:
- "Create a one-page evidence log for a current issue with columns for claim, observation, source, interpretation, alternative, and unresolved gap."
Key point:
- A dated record helps separate new evidence from repeated assumptions.
- Preserve contrary evidence and uncertainty rather than editing the log to fit a conclusion.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
54. Q: How should a person revise a story when new evidence contradicts the original explanation?
A: Show answer
First, confirm that the new evidence is reliable and relevant. Then identify exactly which part of the original story it challenges: an observation, an assumption, a causal connection, or a broad generalization. Update the claim only as far as the evidence requires. Sometimes the new information disproves a conclusion; sometimes it narrows the circumstances in which the conclusion applies. Record what changed and why, and consider whether any decision based on the old story needs to be revisited. Revision is a sign that the reasoning process is functioning, not proof that the person was foolish for holding a provisional view earlier.
Study instruction:
- "Take one belief that has changed and document the evidence that caused the revision and the part of the original story that no longer holds."
Key point:
- Good reasoning is revisable when credible evidence changes.
- Update the specific claim affected rather than swinging automatically to the opposite extreme.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
55. Q: How can a manager test a story that an employee is underperforming because of poor motivation?
A: Show answer
Begin with observable performance data: agreed outputs, deadlines, quality measures, workload, attendance, and documented expectations. Separate these facts from the interpretation that the employee lacks motivation. Examine alternatives such as unclear priorities, inadequate training, resource constraints, conflicting instructions, health or accessibility needs, and dependencies outside the employee's control. Speak with the employee and allow their account to be heard. Compare performance across relevant periods and similar tasks, while avoiding simplistic comparisons that ignore context. Only after the evidence is reviewed should the manager decide what support, clarification, accountability, or formal action is appropriate.
Study instruction:
- "Build a fact-versus-interpretation review for a performance issue and list the evidence needed before attributing motive."
Key point:
- Performance gaps do not by themselves establish a motive or character flaw.
- Fair management tests context and alternative explanations before deciding on action.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
56. Q: How can a team investigate a project delay without adopting a single-cause story?
A: Show answer
Create a timeline of decisions, dependencies, approvals, resource availability, scope changes, supplier actions, and delivery milestones. Distinguish recorded dates from estimates and recollections. Identify whether the delay arose from one critical event or from interacting factors such as late requirements, capacity constraints, rework, and external approvals. Ask what evidence would show the contribution of each factor and whether the proposed explanation fits the sequence. Avoid attributing the delay to one team merely because its work was the most visible. The investigation should end with a proportionate causal account and actions directed at the causes that can be changed.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a project-delay timeline and identify at least three plausible contributing factors before assigning responsibility."
Key point:
- Complex outcomes often have multiple interacting causes.
- A timeline and evidence-based causal review are better than immediate blame.
Source links:
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
57. Q: What common mistakes should be avoided when separating fact from fiction?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include treating a thought as an observation, confusing a feeling with proof of motive, assuming correlation establishes causation, ignoring common causes, selecting only supporting evidence, using a metaphor as a literal description, applying a principle outside its context, and changing language without checking whether the facts have changed. Another mistake is becoming so skeptical that no conclusion can ever be reached. The chapter calls for disciplined testing, not paralysis. A sound conclusion can be provisional and still guide action when its evidence, assumptions, uncertainty, and review point are made clear.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recent decision and check it for unsupported motives, causal leaps, missing context, selective evidence, and overgeneralized language."
Key point:
- The main errors occur when interpretation is mistaken for evidence.
- Avoid both uncritical certainty and endless doubt.
Source links:
- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
58. Q: How can the chapter's method be integrated into a repeatable decision routine?
A: Show answer
Use a short routine before making a consequential judgment: state the question and purpose; write the observable facts with their sources; label interpretations, emotions, predictions, and assumptions; map the proposed causal links; identify missing information and alternative explanations; check whether metaphors or loaded words are narrowing the view; compare the story with current context; and explain the reasoning to an independent reviewer. Decide what action is justified now, what uncertainty remains, and what new evidence should trigger review. This routine makes stories more useful while preserving the ability to change them when reality changes.
Study instruction:
- "Use the routine on one live decision and keep the fact list, causal explanation, uncertainty, and review trigger together."
Key point:
- A repeatable process turns critical thinking into an operational habit.
- Decisions can proceed with stated uncertainty and a plan to revisit the evidence.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
- Rohrer: Thinking Clearly About Correlations and Causation
59. Q: When should reframing a story not be used as a substitute for action or accountability?
A: Show answer
Reframing is not appropriate when it is used to deny verified harm, excuse misconduct, dismiss another person's experience, or avoid a necessary investigation. If records establish a missed control, unsafe condition, discriminatory act, or financial irregularity, changing the wording does not remove the underlying obligation to respond. Likewise, positive language cannot replace resources, training, policy change, or escalation where those are needed. The chapter's method requires facts first. A constructive story must remain compatible with evidence and must lead to proportionate action, including formal procedures when the issue warrants them.
Study instruction:
- "For a difficult issue, identify which parts can be reframed and which verified facts require direct corrective action or formal escalation."
Key point:
- Reframing must never be used to conceal evidence or avoid accountability.
- A more useful story should support responsible action, not replace it.
Source links:
- National Academies: Reproducibility and Replicability in Science
